PDX tumor models were generated in compliance with MSKCC Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee protocol #16-08-011, which requires that mice are euthanized before tumors reach 2000 mm<sup>3</sup> in volume or 2 cm in the largest linear dimension.</p></sec><sec id="Sec12" disp-level="2"><title>Bayesian tensor factorization model for predicting combination drug response</title><p id="Par51">We use a hierarchical generative model that simultaneously models both single and combination drug observations. We treat each drug at each dose individually as a single drug-dose. <inline-formula id="IEq18"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" encourages sparsity and <italic>τ</italic><sub>0</sub> are precisions.</p><p id="Par54">To fit this model, we utilize Gibbs sampling to sample from the posterior distribution, since all of the relevant priors are conditionally conjugate. For the prospective study, the Gibbs samplers were run for 20K steps. Our implementations were done in Python, making use of the numpy<sup><xref rid="CR72" ref-type="bibr">72</xref></sup> and scipy<sup><xref rid="CR73" ref-type="bibr">73</xref></sup> packages.</p></sec><sec id="Sec13" disp-level="2"><title>Bayesian model for predicting combination drug synergy</title><p id="Par55">For the pure synergy modeling setting, we use the following <inline-formula id="IEq19"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M74"><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>s</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:math></inline-formula> is the <italic>n</italic>-th synergy score between drug-doses <italic>i</italic> and <italic>j</italic> on cell line <italic>k</italic>. It is calculated as <inline-formula id="IEq20"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M76"><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>s</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>¯</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>¯</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:math></inline-formula>, where <inline-formula id="IEq21"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M78"><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:math></inline-formula> is the <italic>n</italic>-th observed viability of applying <italic>i</italic> and <italic>j</italic> to <italic>k</italic>, and <inline-formula id="IEq22"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M80"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>¯</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula> is the average observed viability of applying <italic>i</italic> to <italic>k</italic>. When <inline-formula id="IEq23"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M82"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>¯</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula> is not available because drug-dose <italic>i</italic> was not tested directly on <italic>k</italic>, then it is imputed by linear interpolation (in log-concentration space) of neighboring concentrations of the same drug.</p><p id="Par56"><italic>μ</italic><sub><italic>i</italic><italic>j</italic><italic>k</italic></sub> is the mean synergy value of applying <italic>i</italic> and <italic>j</italic> to <italic>k</italic> and <italic>τ</italic> is global observational precision, and <inline-formula id="IEq24"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" prior as its counterpart in the previous model:</p><disp-formula id="Equ19"><label>19</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M86" <inline-formula id="IEq25"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M92"><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">τ</mml:mi><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi it follows a Gamma process prior with elements <italic>γ</italic><sub><italic>s</italic></sub>. The hyper-parameters of the Gamma process are chosen as <italic>a</italic><sub>1</sub> = 2 and <italic>a</italic><sub><italic>s</italic></sub> = 3 for <italic>s</italic> ≥ 2.</p><p id="Par57">The <inline-formula id="IEq27"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M100"><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">λ</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>ℓ</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:math></inline-formula> is a vector of scales for the embedding of drug-dose <italic>i</italic>.</p><p id="Par58">This model is also fit via Gibbs sampling.</p></sec><sec id="Sec14" disp-level="2"><title>Active learning algorithm</title><p id="Par59">Our active learning procedure is a generalization of an optimal active learning procedure called Diameter-based Active Learning (DBAL)<sup><xref rid="CR26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref>,<xref rid="CR27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup>. Our approach applies to general probabilistic models that consist of an experimental space <inline-formula id="IEq28"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M102"><mml:mi class="MJX-tex-caligraphic" mathvariant="script">X</mml:mi></mml:math></inline-formula>, an outcome space <inline-formula id="IEq29"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M104"><mml:mi class="MJX-tex-caligraphic" mathvariant="script">Y</mml:mi></mml:math></inline-formula>, and a set of parameters Θ. We assume that the likelihoods factorize so that for a sequence <inline-formula id="IEq30"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M106"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mo>…</mml:mo><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:mi class="MJX-tex-caligraphic" mathvariant="script">X</mml:mi><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:mi class="MJX-tex-caligraphic" mathvariant="script">Y</mml:mi></mml:math></inline-formula> and parameter <italic>θ</italic> ∈ Θ, we have</p><disp-formula id="Equ24"><label>24</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M108" display="block"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>θ</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mo>…</mml:mo><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>∣</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mo>…</mml:mo><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mo mathsize="big">∏</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>θ</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>∣</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math></disp-formula><p>A <italic>plate</italic> of experiments is a sequence <italic>P</italic> = (<italic>x</italic><sub>1</sub>, …, <italic>x</italic><sub><italic>b</italic></sub>) of experiments <inline-formula id="IEq31"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M110"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:mi class="MJX-tex-caligraphic" mathvariant="script">X</mml:mi></mml:math></inline-formula>. For a corresponding set of outcomes (<italic>y</italic><sub>1</sub>, …, <italic>y</italic><sub><italic>b</italic></sub>), we use the shorthand <italic>y</italic><sub><italic>P</italic></sub> to denote the sequence and <inline-formula id="IEq32"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M112"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>θ</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>P</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>∣</mml:mo><mml:mi>P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∏</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>θ</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>∣</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> to denote the likelihood of the outcome sequence for a given plate.</p><p id="Par60">In the combination drug setting, the parameters <italic>θ</italic> include all of the parameters from the tensor factorization model, i.e., the <italic>μ</italic><sub><italic>i</italic><italic>j</italic><italic>k</italic></sub>’s, the <inline-formula id="IEq33"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M114"><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>ℓ</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:math></inline-formula>’s, etc. The experiment space consists of triples (<italic>i</italic>, <italic>j</italic>, <italic>k</italic>) and pairs (<italic>i</italic>, <italic>k</italic>) where <italic>i</italic> and <italic>j</italic> are drug-doses and <italic>k</italic> is a cell line. Outcomes in this setting are logit-transformed viabilities, and so the outcome space corresponds to the reals, i.e., <inline-formula id="IEq34"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M116"><mml:mi class="MJX-tex-caligraphic" mathvariant="script">Y</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mml:mi></mml:math></inline-formula>.</p><p id="Par61">Given a prior distribution <italic>π</italic> over Θ and a set of observations <inline-formula id="IEq35"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M118"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mo>…</mml:mo><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:mi class="MJX-tex-caligraphic" mathvariant="script">X</mml:mi><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:mi class="MJX-tex-caligraphic" mathvariant="script">Y</mml:mi></mml:math></inline-formula>, the posterior distribution over Θ is given by</p><disp-formula id="Equ25"><label>25</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M120" mathsize="big">∏</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>θ</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>∣</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∫</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi and <italic>H</italic><sub><italic>θ</italic></sub>(<italic>P</italic>).</p><p id="Par63">Since computing <italic>s</italic><sub><italic>n</italic></sub>(<italic>P</italic>) requires integrating over the posterior, we cannot hope to do so directly. Instead, we compute a Monte Carlo approximation of <italic>s</italic><sub><italic>n</italic></sub>(<italic>P</italic>) by sampling sum can be further approximated by sub-sampling triples (<italic>i</italic>, <italic>j</italic>, <italic>k</italic>) and computing the Monte Carlo average only over the selected triples.</p><p id="Par64">Armed with the estimator <inline-formula id="IEq37"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M134"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover our active learning procedure is to enumerate a set of candidate plates <italic>P</italic><sub>1</sub>, …, <italic>P</italic><sub><italic>T</italic></sub> and select the plate <inline-formula id="IEq38"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" with the lowest score <inline-formula id="IEq39"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M138"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover In the supplement, we show that this strategy leads to provably near-optimal guarantees. Specifically, we prove that the convergence rate of BATCHIE is upper-bounded by a function of a problem-specific parameter, called the splitting index, that determines the complexity of the learning problem in the sense that any active learning strategy, regardless of computational power, must have a convergence rate that is lower-bounded by this same splitting index.</p><p id="Par65">To select a batch of plates, we select sequentially. We first select <inline-formula id="IEq40"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M140"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>⋆</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula> as the plate minimizing <inline-formula id="IEq41"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M142"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>s</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>^</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>⋆</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>. Having selected plates <inline-formula id="IEq42"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" where <inline-formula id="IEq45"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" is the plate formed by concatenating the constituent plates <inline-formula id="IEq46"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" Observe that this is equivalent to selecting <inline-formula id="IEq47"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M154"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>⋆</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula> conditioned on having already selected <inline-formula id="IEq48"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M156"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>⋆</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mo>…</mml:mo><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>⋆</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula>. This iterative strategy of optimization has been shown to enjoy strong theoretical guarantees<sup><xref rid="CR74" ref-type="bibr">74</xref>,<xref rid="CR75" ref-type="bibr">75</xref></sup>.</p></sec><sec id="Sec15" disp-level="2"><title>Empirical evaluation of PDBAL</title><p id="Par66">PDBAL was evaluated on several probabilistic regression settings in which the model was parameterized by a coefficient vector <inline-formula id="IEq49"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M158"><mml:mi>θ</mml:mi><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:math></inline-formula>. The regression models include linear regression with homoscedastic Gaussian noise, logistic regression, Poisson regression with the exponential link function, and Beta regression under the mean parameterization<sup><xref rid="CR76" ref-type="bibr">76</xref></sup>:</p><disp-formula id="Equ31"><label>31</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M160" display="block"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>θ</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi><mml:mo>;</mml:mo><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>Beta</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi><mml:mo>∣</mml:mo><mml:mi>ϕ</mml:mi><mml:mi>μ</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>ϕ</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mi>μ</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math></disp-formula><p>where <inline-formula id="IEq50"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M162"><mml:mi>μ</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>e</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mo>⟨</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">θ</mml:mi><mml:mo>⟩</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac></mml:math></inline-formula>, <inline-formula id="IEq51"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M164"><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mml:mi><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:math></inline-formula> is the feature vector, and <italic>ϕ</italic> &gt; 0 is a fixed constant. For all experiments, we used a normal prior distribution on <inline-formula id="IEq52"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M166"><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">θ</mml:mi><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:math></inline-formula> with identity covariance. For the linear regression setting, the posterior was computed in closed form. The other models were implemented in PyStan<sup><xref rid="CR77" ref-type="bibr">77</xref></sup>, and posterior samples were generated by the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS)<sup><xref rid="CR78" ref-type="bibr">78</xref></sup>.</p><p id="Par67">We considered five objectives, specified by a given distance:</p><list list-type="bullet"><list-item><p id="Par68">First coordinate: <inline-formula where <bold><italic>x</italic></bold><sub>1:<italic>d</italic>/2</sub> denotes restriction of the vector <bold><italic>x</italic></bold> to its first <italic>d</italic>/2 coordinates.</p></list-item></list><p id="Par73">We compared against 3 baselines: Random, Var, and EIG. Random chooses uniformly at random from the pool of available queries. Var chooses queries based on maximizing the posterior predictive variance:</p><disp-formula id="Equ32"><label>32</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M182" mathvariant="double-struck">E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>~</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>π</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>[</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow><mml:mo>]</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>[</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>]</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math></disp-formula><p>EIG uses the BALD formulation<sup><xref rid="CR79" ref-type="bibr">79</xref></sup> to maximize expected mutual information between the outcome and the parameter <italic>θ</italic>:</p><disp-formula id="Equ33"><label>33</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M184" display="block"><mml:mi class="MJX-tex-caligraphic" mathvariant="script">I</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi><mml:mo>;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">θ</mml:mi><mml:mo>∣</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>π</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>H</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>π</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="double-struck">E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">θ</mml:mi><mml:mo>~</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>π</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>[</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>H</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>θ</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mo>]</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math></disp-formula><p>where <inline-formula id="IEq60"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M186"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>H</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>π</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> is entropy of the posterior predictive distribution at <bold><italic>x</italic></bold>. For linear regression, this was computed in closed form, while for the other settings, it was approximated numerically.</p><p id="Par74">In all experiments, the ground truth <bold><italic>θ</italic></bold><sup>⋆</sup> was drawn uniformly from vectors of length 2. Data points were drawn from a mixture distribution: with probability 1 − <italic>p</italic>, they were drawn uniformly from vectors of length 1, and with probability <italic>p</italic>, each coordinate was set to 0 with probability 1/<italic>d</italic> and the remaining coordinates drawn so the resulting vector has length 1. For some objectives, this sparse distribution is particularly informative. In all simulations, <italic>d</italic> = 10 and <italic>p</italic> = 1/10.</p></sec><sec id="Sec16" disp-level="2"><title>Retrospective simulations</title><sec id="Sec17" disp-level="3"><title>Data retrieval and preparation</title><p id="Par75">We downloaded the ALMANAC<sup><xref rid="CR5" ref-type="bibr">5</xref></sup>, GDSC<sup>2</sup><sup><xref rid="CR6" ref-type="bibr">6</xref></sup>, and MERCK<sup><xref rid="CR7" ref-type="bibr">7</xref></sup> datasets from their respective sources (see Data Availability). For the MERCK dataset, viabilities were provided. For the ALMANAC dataset, PercentGrowth values were provided. We converted these to viability scores using the formula</p><disp-formula id="Equ34"><label>34</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M188" display="block"><mml:mtable><mml:mtr><mml:mtd columnalign="right"><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>viability</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>PercentGrowth</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>100</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>200</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:mtd></mml:mtr></mml:mtable></mml:math></disp-formula><p>For the GDSC<sup>2</sup> dataset, well intensity values were provided, along with high control and low control intensities. These were converted to viability scores using the same formula as in the prospective study. For all studies, replicates were averaged to produce a single viability measurement for all recorded cell line, drug-dose 1, drug-dose 2 triplets. Drug-doses that were not present in combination experiments were dropped.</p></sec><sec id="Sec18" disp-level="3"><title>Plate and random holdout construction</title><p id="Par76">From the viability measurements, we constructed synthetic plates to closely match the plates used in the prospective sarcoma study. Drug-doses were randomly divided into groups of 20, and initial plates we constructed by considering each cell line <italic>c</italic> and each pair of groups <inline-formula id="IEq61"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M190"><mml:mi>g</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:math></inline-formula> and collecting all measurements satisfying that the cell line is in <italic>c</italic>, one of the drug-doses is in <italic>g</italic>, and the other drug-dose is in <inline-formula id="IEq62"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M192"><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:math></inline-formula>. Due to the biased sampling of drug combinations in the three experimental designs, this resulted in plates of unequal sizes. We corrected for this by greedily merging the two smallest plates within a cell line until a minimum size threshold was met. We then performed a single pass through the plates for each cell line and merged the largest plate with the smallest plate. To align the plate sizes, we selected a threshold and dropped all plates smaller than the threshold and dropped observations from plates larger than the threshold until they had the same number of observations. The threshold was chosen to minimize the total number of observations removed. In each simulation, a random holdout set was created by subsampling 10% of the observations from every plate.</p></sec><sec id="Sec19" disp-level="3"><title>Experimental design</title><p id="Par77">For a given dataset and plate construction, we first created an initial covering plate by randomly selecting observations to greedily cover the cell lines and drug-doses. Given the same dataset, set of plates, and initial covering plate, we ran both the Random and BATCHIE methods. At each round of data collection, the methods picked <italic>K</italic> cell lines and 3 plates per cell line. <italic>K</italic> was selected to be 1/10th the number of cell lines in the dataset, rounded down (<italic>K</italic> = 6 for ALMANAC, <italic>K</italic> = 12 for GDSC<sup>2</sup>, and <italic>K</italic> = 3 for MERCK).</p></sec><sec id="Sec20" disp-level="3"><title>Posterior inference</title><p id="Par78">At each round, before selecting plates, 200 MCMC samples were drawn from the current posterior using 5 parallel chains, each with a burn-in period of 2000 steps and a thinning factor of 40. These samples were then used to evaluate accuracy on the holdout set and, for BATCHIE, used to select the next set of plates to observe. For each dataset, we ran the plate construction and simulation using 25 different random seeds.</p></sec><sec id="Sec21" disp-level="3"><title>Cell line restrictions</title><p id="Par79">For the cell line restriction simulations, cell lines were subsampled uniformly at random. The number of cell lines chosen per round was adjusted accordingly (1/10th of the number of selected cell lines). The number of plates per cell line in each round remained 3.</p></sec><sec id="Sec22" disp-level="3"><title>Metrics</title><p id="Par80">For each dataset construction, we trained a model using the full training set, i.e., everything except for the holdout validation set, by drawing 200 MCMC samples from the posterior using 5 parallel chains, each with a burn-in period of 2000 steps and a thinning factor of 40. Given a holdout set of experiment/viability pairs <inline-formula id="IEq63"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" and a candidate predictor <inline-formula id="IEq65"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M198"><mml:mi>f</mml:mi><mml:mo>:</mml:mo><mml:mi class="MJX-tex-caligraphic" the normalized accuracy is given by the ratio of <italic>R</italic><sup>2</sup> scores:</p><disp-formula id="Equ35"><label>35</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M200" <inline-formula id="IEq66"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M204"><mml:mi>ȳ</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∑</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula> is the empirical mean of observed viabilities.</p><p id="Par81">Efficiency gains/batches saved and experiments saved were computed by calculating the holdout <italic>R</italic><sup>2</sup> of the BATCHIE-trained model (at round 15, unless otherwise specified) and then searching for the earliest round at which a Random-trained model had comparable performance. BATCHIE and Random models were only compared within the same random seed, and therefore only on the same dataset and plate set construction.</p><p id="Par82">As none of the retrospective datasets have control/non-cancer lines, we calculated TI values by taking all pairwise differences across cell lines. To calculate the Average TI @ Top 20, we computed all TI values from the models’ mean viability predictions, selected the top 20 predicted TI hits, and averaged the corresponding ground truth TI values. To calculate the Top hit AUC, we computed the AUC of the ROC curve, where the ground truth labels correspond to whether or not the TI value occurs in the top 99th percentile of TIs and the predicted values are the predicted TIs formed from the models’ mean viability predictions.</p></sec><sec id="Sec23" disp-level="3"><title>Baseline comparisons</title><p id="Par83">In addition to the Random baseline, which chooses plates uniformly at random subject to the experimental design constraints, we also compared against strategies that maximize the expected information gain (EIG) and posterior predictive variance (Variance). We used the same formulations of these strategies as in the regression comparison setting to calculate the scores of individual wells and averaged across wells to score the corresponding plate.</p></sec><sec id="Sec24" disp-level="3"><title>PDBAL distances</title><p id="Par84">The default distance we use for PDBAL with normal likelihoods is the mean-squared distance over predicted mean viabilities (MSD). Given two sequences of mean viability predictions over the same set of wells (<italic>y</italic><sub>1</sub>, …, <italic>y</italic><sub><italic>n</italic></sub>) and <inline-formula id="IEq67"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M206"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mo>…</mml:mo><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, the MSD is calculated as</p><disp-formula id="Equ37"><label>37</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M208" display="block"><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∑</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math></disp-formula><p>For the retrospective datasets, the prediction set is the set of all cell line, drug-dose 1, drug-dose 2 triplets covered in the dataset. For the prospective study, the prediction set is the set of all possible triplets spanned by the cell lines and drug-doses in the dataset.</p><p id="Par85">For the TI-aware distance (TID), we transform mean viability predictions to TI predictions by computing all pairwise differences across cell lines. For two sets of TI predictions, (<italic>t</italic><sub>1</sub>, …, <italic>t</italic><sub><italic>n</italic></sub>) and <inline-formula id="IEq68"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M210"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mo>…</mml:mo><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, we select the top <italic>K</italic> indices from each to get (<italic>i</italic><sub>1</sub>, …, <italic>i</italic><sub><italic>K</italic></sub>) and <inline-formula id="IEq69"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M212"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mo>…</mml:mo><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>K</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>. The TID is then given by</p><disp-formula id="Equ38"><label>38</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M214" display="block"><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">max</mml:mi><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>K</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∑</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>K</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>K</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mo mathsize="big">∑</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>K</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:math></disp-formula><p>In our simulations, we chose <italic>K</italic> = 20.</p></sec></sec><sec id="Sec25" disp-level="2"><title>Pediatric sarcoma combination screen</title><sec id="Sec26" disp-level="3"><title>Study design</title><p id="Par86">The prospective sarcoma study consisted of 15 rounds of data collection. At each round, different subsets of cell lines were available based on doubling time and the extent to which they had been used in previous rounds (Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="supplementary-material">3c</xref>). Adaptive batches were constrained to 3 combination plates each for 3 available cell lines. In the first round and the eleventh round, unseen cell lines were introduced and the plates were selected to greedily cover unseen drug-doses and drug-dose combinations over the new cell lines. In the remaining rounds, plates were selected using the BATCHIE active learning procedure outlined above with the constraint that 3 separate cell lines be chosen, leading to 9 selected plates in total.</p><p id="Par87">In rounds 1–10, each plate was run singly. During the phase I validation, we identified that BATCHIE was sensitive to undetectable random well failures producing corrupted data. To address this, in rounds 11–15, plates were run in duplicate and quality control checks flagged wells whose duplicates differed by more than 0.5 in viability.</p></sec><sec id="Sec27" disp-level="3"><title>Combination plates</title><p id="Par88">Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="supplementary-material">3a</xref> shows the general schematic for our combination plate setup. Plates consisted of 384 wells with 16 rows and 24 columns. For row plates, 15 of the rows consist of a single drug applied to each of the corresponding column wells at a particular concentration, except two columns that corresponded to high control and low control. The remaining row of the row plate was filled with Dimethylsulfoxide (DMSO), also with two control columns. For column plates, 21 of the columns consist of a single drug applied to each of the corresponding row wells at a particular concentration. The remaining three columns are the high and low control columns (aligned to spatially match the corresponding high/low columns in the row plate) and a column filled only with DMSO.</p><p id="Par89">When a row and column plate are combined, the resulting combination plate contains 15 × 21 = 315 wells that correspond to all combinations of the constituent drug-doses, 15 + 21 = 36 wells that correspond to all single drug-doses, 15 high-control wells, and 15 low-control wells.</p><p id="Par90">Our drug library consisted of 206 drugs, with four of the drugs duplicated to allow for the resulting 210 drugs to be evenly divided over 14 row plates and 10 column plates. Each row and column plate was constructed at 2 different doses: 0.1 μM and 1 μM, leading to a total of 28 row plate choices and 20 column plate choices.</p><p id="Par91">Finally, a full plate consists of a cell line, a row plate, and a column plate. Viabilities for a non-control well <italic>w</italic> are calculated as</p><disp-formula id="Equ39"><label>39</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M216" display="block"><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>viability</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>well</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>count</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>well</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>average</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>low</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>-</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>control</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>count</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>average</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>high</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>-</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>control</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>count</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>average</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>low</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>-</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>control</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>count</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac></mml:math></disp-formula><p>where count (well <italic>w</italic>) is the reading at well <italic>w</italic> and average high/low-control counts are the averages of the readings at the corresponding high/low-control wells.</p></sec><sec id="Sec28" disp-level="3"><title>Therapeutic index</title><p id="Par92">We define the in vitro therapeutic index (TI) to be a differential score that compares two groups of viabilities: one for the target set of cell lines and one for the control set. A high TI corresponds to low viability for most target cells and high viability for all control cells. To calculate TI, we take the difference between the minimum viability on the control lines and the median viability on the target lines: </p><disp-formula id="Equ40"><label>40</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M218" display="block"><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>TI</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>minimum</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>V</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mo>;</mml:mo><mml:mi>C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>for</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>Controls</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>median</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>V</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mo>;</mml:mo><mml:mi>T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>for</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>Target</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math></disp-formula><p>where V(<italic>i</italic>, <italic>j</italic>; <italic>N</italic>) is the viability of drug-dose pair (<italic>i</italic>, <italic>j</italic>) on cell line <italic>N</italic>.</p></sec><sec id="Sec29" disp-level="3"><title>Model visualizations</title><p id="Par93">To determine if BATCHIE discerned the relative mechanistic similarities and differences of the drugs tested in an unsupervised manner, we labeled each drug assessed with simplified mechanisms of action (MoAs) and used low-rank approximation and dimensionality reduction to visualize resulting clusters. We used MoAs from the Genomics of Drug Sensitivity in Cancer (GDSC) database<sup><xref rid="CR80" ref-type="bibr">80</xref></sup> to annotate the 98 drugs assessed in both our study and the GDSC. For the remaining 112 drugs in our library that were not in the GDSC, we manually added MoAs that conformed to the GDSC categories. Mechanisms were adjudicated according to drugs’ International Nonproprietary Name (INN) suffixes, documented mechanisms in the literature, and U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) package inserts for approved molecules. All drugs were categorized as belonging to one of nineteen MoA categories.</p><p id="Par94">Predicted viabilities of the drugs administered as monotherapies were logit-transformed and normalized to a zero-one range. Non-negative matrix factorization (NMF) was performed using the Python package scikit-learn’s NMF module at a random initialization<sup><xref rid="CR81" ref-type="bibr">81</xref></sup>. The decomposed feature matrix represented latent embeddings of the drugs, where each dosage and drug combination was treated as a unique entry, and the coefficient matrix represented latent embeddings of the cell lines tested. By assessing the mean squared error of the resulting low-rank approximation, we determined 15 to be the optimal number of latent components using the elbow method as implemented in the kneed Python package<sup><xref rid="CR82" ref-type="bibr">82</xref></sup>. For plotting purposes, we reduced the dimensionality of the feature matrix using t-distributed stochastic neighbor embedding (t-SNE) as implemented in the scikit-learn t-SNE module<sup><xref rid="CR81" ref-type="bibr">81</xref></sup>. Results from this analysis are shown for high dose (1.0 μM) drugs in Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="supplementary-material">4e</xref>.</p><p id="Par95">To determine if BATCHIE also discerned more fine-grained drug MoAs, we selected 8 more refined molecular mechanisms or pharmaceutical classes that were recurrent among the drugs evaluated. We then identified 2-3 drugs belonging to each such group. We calculated the Pearson correlation coefficients between the BATCHIE-predicted combination therapy viabilities for each drug at both doses assessed. We plotted the resulting hierarchically clustered heatmap using the Python package Seaborn’s clustermap function<sup><xref rid="CR83" ref-type="bibr">83</xref></sup>. These results are show in Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="supplementary-material">4d</xref>.</p></sec><sec id="Sec30" disp-level="3"><title>Ex vivo analysis</title><p id="Par96">For six of the drug combinations from the top EWS hits, we collected dose-response data on 2 additional patient-derived cell lines. The drugs were tested on a regular grid spanning 0.02nM - 1000nM, and the plates were run in duplicates.</p><p id="Par97">As the concentrations tested in this stage did not align with the concentrations tested in the top hit validation, we performed linear interpolation of the mean observed viabilities (in <inline-formula id="IEq70"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M220"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>log</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula> concentration space), in order to compute TI scores with respect to the control data observed in the top hit validation analysis.</p></sec><sec id="Sec31" disp-level="3"><title>Triplet analysis</title><p id="Par98">For our triplet studies, we collected dose-response data along a regular grid (0.1 nM–400 nM for the Ewing study and 0.02 nM–1 μM for the osteosarcoma study). Plates were run in duplicate.</p><p id="Par99">For each cell line and triplet of drugs under consideration, we fit a multi-dimensional isotonic regression to the observed viabilities, restricting the regressed variables to monotonically decrease as a function of dose. Mathematically, we solved</p><disp-formula id="Equ41"><label>41</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M222" display="block"><mml:mspace For any candidate set of concentrations, we linearly (in log-concentration space) interpolated its viability from the smoothed values <inline-formula id="IEq72"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M226"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>^</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula>.</p></sec><sec id="Sec32" disp-level="3"><title>Gene expression and CRISPR knockout analysis</title><p id="Par100">Both the RNA expression data and CRISPR knockout data were obtained from the DepMap data portal<sup><xref rid="CR84" ref-type="bibr">84</xref></sup>. The RNA expression data is represented as a matrix <inline-formula id="IEq73"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M228"><mml:mi>X</mml:mi><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:mi>G</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:math></inline-formula>, where <italic>N</italic> is the number of DepMap cell lines, <italic>G</italic> is the number of protein coding genes in the DepMap library, and <inline-formula id="IEq74"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M230"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>log</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mstyle><mml:mtext>TPM</mml:mtext></mml:mstyle></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, where TPM<sub><italic>c</italic>,<italic>g</italic></sub> is the transcripts per million (TPM) for gene <italic>g</italic> in cell line <italic>c</italic>. Z-scores for the gene expression data were computed by standardizing over cell lines, i.e.,</p><disp-formula id="Equ42"><label>42</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M232" display="block"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>Z</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>¯</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msqrt><mml:mrow><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mo mathsize="big">∑</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>¯</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:msqrt></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math></disp-formula><p>where <inline-formula id="IEq75"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M234"><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo>¯</mml:mo></mml:mover><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∑</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula>.</p><p id="Par101">The CRISPR knockout data is represented as a matrix <inline-formula id="IEq76"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M236"><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">Y</mml:mi><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:mi>G</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:math></inline-formula>, where <italic>Y</italic><sub><italic>c</italic>,<italic>g</italic></sub> is a harmonized score representing the effect of knocking out gene <italic>g</italic> in cell line <italic>c</italic>.</p></sec></sec><sec id="Sec33" disp-level="2"><title>Rational combination analysis</title><p id="Par102">All drug mechanism data was obtained from the Genomics of Drug Sensitivity in Cancer (GDSC) portal<sup><xref rid="CR80" ref-type="bibr">80</xref></sup>. Drugs that do not have GDSC mechanism of action (MoA) annotations were not included in the analysis.</p><sec id="Sec34" disp-level="3"><title>Mechanism of action analysis</title><p id="Par103">Using the GDSC<sup>2</sup> dataset, a single dose-response curve for each drug was formed by considering each observed dose and taking the 5th percentile of mean viabilities over observed cell lines. For each drug, we computed its IC<sub>50</sub> concentration over its corresponding dose-response curve, discarding drugs for which no concentrations had corresponding values below 0.5.</p><p id="Par104">For each drug <italic>i</italic>, we considered all cell lines <italic>j</italic> and drugs <italic>k</italic> at concentrations <italic>c</italic> that co-occured with <italic>i</italic> on <italic>j</italic> in the dataset to form the tensor <inline-formula id="IEq77"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M238"><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">V</mml:mi><mml:mo>∈</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">drug</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">cell</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">drug</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>×</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">conc</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:math></inline-formula> where <italic>V</italic><sub><italic>i</italic><italic>j</italic><italic>k</italic><italic>c</italic></sub> is the log-concentration interpolated mean viability of combining drug <italic>i</italic> at its IC<sub>50</sub> concentration with drug <italic>k</italic> at concentration <italic>c</italic> on cell line <italic>j</italic>, wherever it is validly computable from the data. For two drugs <italic>i</italic>, <inline-formula id="IEq78"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M240"><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:math></inline-formula>, their distance is computed as</p><disp-formula id="Equ43"><label>43</label><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M242" display="block"><mml:mi>d</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo mathsize="big">∑</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>∣</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>V</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>V</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>∣</mml:mo><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math></disp-formula><p>where the sum is taken over only those values for which both <italic>V</italic><sub><italic>i</italic><italic>j</italic><italic>k</italic><italic>c</italic></sub> and <inline-formula id="IEq79"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M244"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>V</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>′</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula> are valid, and <italic>n</italic> is the total number of such valid values.</p></sec><sec id="Sec35" disp-level="3"><title>CROCS analysis</title><p id="Par105">Combinations of drugs in which both members belonged to the ‘clinically relevant oncology combination screen’ (CROCS) for non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC)<sup><xref rid="CR66" ref-type="bibr">66</xref></sup> were classified as CROCS combinations. All other combinations of drugs were identified as non-CROCS combinations. For both the ALMANAC dataset and GDSC<sup>2</sup> dataset, wherever feasible, we computed an 8 × 8 evenly-spaced (in <inline-formula id="IEq80"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M246"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>log</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula>-concentration space) dose-response matrix by linearly interpolating mean observed viabilities. For every pair of drugs <italic>i</italic> and <italic>j</italic> and every pair of cell lines <italic>c</italic> and <italic>t</italic>, we calculated the TI of <italic>i</italic> and <italic>j</italic> where <italic>c</italic> is control and <italic>t</italic> as</p><disp-formula id="Equb"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M248" display="block"><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mi>I</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mo>;</mml:mo><mml:mi>c</mml:mi><mml:mo>;</mml:mo><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>max</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>V</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>−</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>V</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></disp-formula><p>where <inline-formula id="IEq81"><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M250"><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>V</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math></inline-formula> is the interpolated viability of cell line <italic>c</italic> under drug <italic>i</italic> at dose <italic>d</italic><sub><italic>i</italic></sub> and drug <italic>j</italic> and dose <italic>d</italic><sub><italic>j</italic></sub> and the maximum ranges over the dose grids for drugs <italic>i</italic> and <italic>j</italic>, respectively. For ALMANAC, we only considered those TI values for which the target cell line was NSCLC and the control cell line was not NSCLC, while for GDSC<sup>2</sup> we considered all possible TI values.</p></sec></sec><sec id="Sec36" disp-level="2"><title>Data visualizations</title><p id="Par106">All plots were made in Python using the matplotlib<sup><xref rid="CR85" ref-type="bibr">85</xref></sup> and seaborn<sup><xref rid="CR83" ref-type="bibr">83</xref></sup> packages.</p></sec><sec id="Sec37" disp-level="2"><title>Statistical analyses</title><p id="Par107">All Mann-Whitney <italic>U</italic>-tests, Spearman’s rho calculations, Pearson’s rho calculations, Pearson’s chi-square calculations were computed using Scipy’s<sup><xref rid="CR73" ref-type="bibr">73</xref></sup>stats package, using the default settings. All p-values from Mann-Whitney U-tests, Spearman’s rho tests, and Pearson’s rho tests were computed using two-sided alternatives. The permutation test in Fig. <xref rid="Fig4" ref-type="fig">4</xref>g was custom coded. Unless otherwise noted, all tests were performed independently of each other.</p></sec><sec id="Sec38" disp-level="2"><title>Wetlab protocols</title><sec id="Sec39" disp-level="3"><title>Cell lines</title><p id="Par108">Established cell lines (SJSA-1, U2OS, SAOS-2, MG-63, A673, SKNEP, MDA-MB-231, RPE and BJ) were obtained from the American Type Culture Collection (ATCC). TC-71 was obtained from the Children’s Oncology Group cell line repository. Wit49 was provided courtesy of Dr. Herman Yeger (Toronto, Canada). Kelly was obtained from the DSMZ-German Collection of Microorganisms and Cell Cultures GmbH. MSKEWS-83311, MSKEWS-83033, MSKEWS-38338, MSKEWS-66647, MSKOST-11890, and MSKRMS-12808 were generated from patient-derived xenograft (PDX) tumor tissue established from patient tumors treated at Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center (MSKCC). Tumor tissue was dissociated utilizing the Human Tumor Dissociation Kit (Miltenyi Biotec) according to manufacturer’s recommendations or using an in-house combination of Collagenase Type II (Gibco), Dispase II (Millipore Sigma), Deoxyribonuclease I (Millipore Sigma) in DMEM (Gibco) containing 10% fetal bovine serum (Corning), followed by mechanical dissociation with Macs dissociator (Miltenyi). MSKCC patients provided informed and signed consent and enrolled onto a tumor profiling research study (Genomic profiling in cancer patients; <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/ct2/show/NCT01775072" ext-link-type="uri">NCT01775072</ext-link>) approved by the MSKCC Institutional Review Board under protocol IRB#12-245, #06-107, and #17-387 to enable tumor cell line generation. PDX tumor models were generated under MSKCC Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee protocol #16-08-011. All PDX-derived samples utilized NGS mice (NOD.Cg-Prkdcscid Il2rgtm1Wjl/SzJ, Jackson Labs Strain #005557), aged between 5 and 8 weeks. Mouse sex was not recorded. Additional cell line information and culture conditions are detailed in Supplementary Data <xref rid="MOESM4" ref-type="supplementary-material">1</xref>. Media was supplemented with 1% Anti-Anti (Gibco) at the time of drug screening. Authentication of established cell lines by short tandem repeat (STR) was performed. Validation and authentication of PDX-derived cell lines were accomplished by next-generation targeted sequencing using MSK-IMPACT<sup><xref rid="CR86" ref-type="bibr">86</xref></sup> and matched with source patient tumor. Optimal seeding densities for drug screens were empirically determined for each cell line.</p><p id="Par109">Sex information of the cell lines is shown in Supplementary Data <xref rid="MOESM4" ref-type="supplementary-material">1</xref>. Sex information for publicly available cell lines was taken from Cellosaurus. Sex information for PDX-derived cell lines was self-reported. Sex was not considered in the study design. Samples were grouped together based on cancer subtype, with insufficient sample sizes to distinguish differences between female- and male-derived samples due to the unique functional profile of each cell line and ex vivo sample.</p></sec><sec id="Sec40" disp-level="3"><title>Drugs</title><p id="Par110">The studies were comprised of 206 drugs obtained from multiple sources including Selleckchem, MedChemExpress, Sigma-Aldrich, Tocris, LKT laboratories Inc., Xcess Biosciences, and the National Cancer Institute (NCI) Division of Cancer Treatment and Diagnosis. A list of all screened chemicals and sources are provided in Supplementary Data <xref rid="MOESM5" ref-type="supplementary-material">2</xref>.</p></sec><sec id="Sec41" disp-level="3"><title>BATCHIE plates</title><p id="Par111">All assay plates contained baseline viability (high control) and complete cell killing controls (low control) consisting of 1% DMSO (v/v) and 1 μM “killer mix”, a proprietary mixture of cytotoxic drugs at 1% DMSO (v/v), respectively. Phase I and phase II drugs were prepared in 100% DMSO (v/v) and added onto 384-well microplates to generate the “200X source plates” with drugs at a concentration of either 200 μM or 20 μM. To facilitate combination testing, drugs were arrayed in a “row” or “column” format with “row plates” consisting of 15 drugs arrayed in rows per plate and “column plates” consisting of 21 drugs arrayed in columns per plate with the same drug concentration for each drug in both plate configurations. Drugs were combined using the Apricot Designs Personal Pipettor (SPT Labtech) which mixes a row plate with a column plate and water into a new 384-well plate to generate a “10X intermediate plate” of 10 μM or 1 μM in 10% DMSO (v/v). Subsequently, the 10X intermediate plates are stamped into “assay plates” to be combined with cells generating the final 1X concentration of 1 μM or 0.1 μM in 1% DMSO (v/v) (Supplementary Figs. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="supplementary-material">11</xref>,  <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="supplementary-material">12a</xref>).</p><p id="Par112">For validation studies, 2-drug combinations were evaluated for random unseen combinations in five osteosarcoma, five Ewing sarcoma, and two non-cancer cell lines; high predicted therapeutic index (TI) in five Ewing sarcoma and two non-cancer cell lines; and Ewing sarcoma ex vivo in two Ewing sarcomas (Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="supplementary-material">11</xref>). 7-doses (Plate 1) and 9-doses (Plate 2) 4-fold serial dilutions were prepared into 96-well plates to generate 20X source plates at 8 μM or 20 μM in 10% DMSO (v/v) as the highest concentrations. The plates were further consolidated and combined into 384-well 10X intermediate plates at 4 μM or 10 μM in 10% DMSO (v/v). The 10X intermediate plates were then transferred into assay plates along with cells to produce a 1X concentration of 0.4 μM or 1 μM in 1% DMSO (v/v) (Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="supplementary-material">12b</xref>).</p><p id="Par113">Higher order validation plates for Ewing sarcoma and osteosarcoma studies (Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="supplementary-material">11</xref>) were performed by first generating 7-doses (Drug 1), 5-doses (Drug 2) and 5-doses (Drug 3) 4-fold serial dilutions, obtaining 30X source plates at 12 μM or 30 μM in 10% DMSO (v/v) as the highest concentrations. The three 30X source plates were combined to create the 10X intermediate plates with maximum concentrations of 4 μM or 10 μM in 10% DMSO (v/v) and stamped to combine with cells to yield 1X assay plates of 0.4 μM or 1 μM in 1% DMSO (v/v) (Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="supplementary-material">12c</xref>). Five Ewing sarcoma and two non-cancer cell lines were used for the Ewing sarcoma validation studies and five osteosarcoma lines for the osteosarcoma studies.</p><p id="Par114">For osteosarcoma studies validating agents with high predicted therapeutic indices (TI), drugs were plated in 384-well 20X source microplates using the row and column format described above for phase I and phase II (Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="supplementary-material">11</xref>) at 20 μM or 2 μM in 10% DMSO (v/v). The sources plates were combined into 10X intermediate plates yielding 10 μM or 1 μM in 10% DMSO (v/v). Assay plates were stamped from the 10X intermediate plate and combined with cells for a final drug concentration of 1 μM (high concentration) or 0.1 μM (low concentration) in 1% DMSO (v/v). All possible two drug combinations were obtained—high/high, high/low, low/high and low/low. Five osteosarcoma and two non-cancer cell lines were used.</p></sec><sec id="Sec42" disp-level="3"><title>Cytotoxicity assay</title><p id="Par115">Cells were plated at their optimized seeding densities and in their corresponding media (Supplementary Data <xref rid="MOESM4" ref-type="supplementary-material">1</xref>) into 384-well clear-bottom black assay plates (BATCHIE plates) using the MultiDrop® 384 dispenser (Thermo Fisher Scientific). After incubating cells in drug for 72 h at 37 <sup>∘</sup>C and 5% CO2 in a Heracell™ 240i incubator (Thermo Fisher Scientific), Alamar Blue (Sigma-Aldrich) is added using the MultiDrop™ Combi 384 dispenser (Thermo Fisher Scientific) and incubated for another 24 h. Fluorescence signal readout was acquired using a Cytation™ 5 multimode reader (Agilent Biotek) using the monochromator with an excitation of 555/20 nm and emission of 596/20 nm. A summary of the screening protocol is available in Supplementary Data <xref rid="MOESM6" ref-type="supplementary-material">3</xref>.</p></sec></sec><sec id="Sec43" disp-level="2"><title>Reporting summary</title><p id="Par116">Further information on research design is available in the <xref rid="MOESM7" ref-type="supplementary-material">Nature Portfolio Reporting Summary</xref> linked to this article.</p></sec></sec><sec id="Sec44" disp-level="1"><title>Supplementary information</title>

A peer review file is available.</p></sec></sec><sec id="notes3" disp-level="1"><title>Data availability</title><p>Source data are provided with this paper. Original ALMANAC data is publicly available at <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://wiki.nci.nih.gov/download/attachments/338237347/ComboDrugGrowth_Nov2017.zip" ext-link-type="uri">https://wiki.nci.nih.gov/download/attachments/338237347/ComboDrugGrowth_Nov2017.zip</ext-link>. Original GDSC<sup>2</sup> data is publicly available at Figshare [10.6084/m9.figshare.19141916.v1, 10.6084/m9.figshare.19141922.v1]. Original MERCK data is publicly available at Figshare [10.1158/1535-7163.22503133.v1, 10.1158/1535-7163.22503133.v1]. Our postprocessed form of the these publicly available data is available at Zenodo [<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://zenodo.org/records/12764821" ext-link-type="uri">https://zenodo.org/records/12764821</ext-link>]. The prospective data generated in this study are available at Zenodo [10.5281/zenodo.13871987]. Sequencing data collected on PDX-derived cell lines is not available, as the IRB did not consent patients to release identifiable health information. <xref rid="Sec45" ref-type="sec">Source data</xref> are provided with this paper.</p></sec><sec id="notes4" disp-level="1"><title>Code availability</title><p>BATCHIE code is open source under an MIT license and publicly available at <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://github.com/tansey-lab/batchie" ext-link-type="uri">https://github.com/tansey-lab/batchie</ext-link>, and also at Zenodo<sup><xref rid="CR87" ref-type="bibr">87</xref></sup>.</p></sec><sec id="FPar2" disp-level="1"><title>Competing interests</title><p id="Par118">The Authors declare the following competing interests. J.B.W. has contract employment and equity interest in SpringWorks Therapeutics. F.D.C. receives institutional research support from Eisai Co. and Y-mAbs Therapeutics. A.L.K. is on the Scientific Advisory Board of Emendo Biotherapeutics, Karyopharm Therapeutics, Imago BioSciences, and DarwinHealth; is co-Founder and on the Scientific Advisory Board of Isabl; has equity interest in Imago BioSciences, Emendo Biotherapeutics and Isabl; and receives royalty income from Labcorp. The remaining authors declare no competing interests.</p></sec><sec id="fn-group1" sec-type="fn-group" disp-level="1"><title>Footnotes</title><fn-group><fn id="fn1"><p><bold>Publisher’s note</bold> Springer Nature remains neutral with regard to jurisdictional claims in published maps and institutional affiliations.</p></fn></fn-group></sec><sec id="sec46" disp-level="1"><title>Supplementary information</title><p>The online version contains supplementary material available at 10.1038/s41467-024-55287-7.</p></sec><sec id="Bib1" sec-type="ref-list" disp-level="1"><title>References</title><sec id="Bib1_sec2" disp-level="2"><ref-list><ref id="CR1"><label>1.</label><mixed-citation id="mc-CR1"><named-content content-type="citation-string">Clavel, François &amp; Hance, A. xml:lang="en" sec-type="data-availability-statement" disp-level="2"><title>Data Availability Statement</title><p>Source data are provided with this paper. Original ALMANAC data is publicly available at <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://wiki.nci.nih.gov/download/attachments/338237347/ComboDrugGrowth_Nov2017.zip" ext-link-type="uri">https://wiki.nci.nih.gov/download/attachments/338237347/ComboDrugGrowth_Nov2017.zip</ext-link>. Original GDSC<sup>2</sup> data is publicly available at Figshare [10.6084/m9.figshare.19141916.v1, 10.6084/m9.figshare.19141922.v1]. Original MERCK data is publicly available at Figshare [10.1158/1535-7163.22503133.v1, 10.1158/1535-7163.22503133.v1]. Our postprocessed form of the these publicly available data is available at Zenodo [<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://zenodo.org/records/12764821" ext-link-type="uri">https://zenodo.org/records/12764821</ext-link>]. The prospective data generated in this study are available at Zenodo [10.5281/zenodo.13871987]. Sequencing data collected on PDX-derived cell lines is not available, as the IRB did not consent patients to release identifiable health information. <xref rid="Sec45" ref-type="sec">Source data</xref> are provided with this paper.</p><p>BATCHIE code is open source under an MIT license and publicly available at <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://github.com/tansey-lab/batchie" ext-link-type="uri">https://github.com/tansey-lab/batchie</ext-link>, and also at Zenodo<sup><xref rid="CR87" ref-type="bibr">87</xref></sup>.</p></sec></sec></body></article>
